\hypersetup{
  pdftitle={The Sphere Packing Problem in Dimension 4 and the Twenty-Four-Cell Conjecture},
  pdfauthor={Deep Bhattacharjee, Ushashi Bhattacharya, Priyabrata Mandal, Shounak Bhattacharya},
  pdfsubject={Sphere packing in dimension four},

\begin{abstract}
We prove that every Voronoi cell of a unit-ball packing in $\mathbb{R}^4$ has
at least the volume of the regular twenty-four-cell, with equality only for
$D_4$, unless $24$ to $28$ other centres lie within $\sqrt6$ of its centre,
not all at distance $2$. Hence the twenty-four-cell conjecture holds for every
contact configuration, $D_4$ is a strict local minimum of the cell volume, and
no periodic packing avoiding the exception is denser than $D_4$; the remaining
cells reduce to two explicit inequalities about at most twenty-eight centres.
The cells alone bound the density of every packing of unit balls in
$\mathbb{R}^4$ by $0.63527$, below the three-point bound.
\end{abstract}

\maketitle

\tableofcontents

\section{Introduction}\label{sec:intro}

The sphere packing problem in dimension four asks for the supremum
$\Delta_4$ of the densities of packings of unit balls in $\mathbb{R}^4$.
The $D_4$ root lattice, or checkerboard lattice, scaled so that nearest
centres lie at distance $2$, packs the balls at density
$\pi^2/16=0.61685\ldots$, and by a theorem of Korkine and Zolotareff
\cite{KZ1872} no lattice packing of $\mathbb{R}^4$ is denser. Whether a
packing that is not a lattice can be denser is open.\footnote{The lattice
  problem is a problem about positive definite quadratic forms: the densest
  lattice packing in dimension $n$ corresponds to the form of given minimum
  with the least determinant, and Korkine and Zolotareff found it for
  $n=4$ and $n=5$ \cite{KZ1872}. Blichfeldt settled $n=6,7,8$ in the
  1920s and 1930s, and Cohn and Kumar $n=24$ \cite{CK09}. Among all
  packings, periodic or not, the answer is known in dimensions $1$, $2$,
  $3$ \cite{Thu10,FT42,Hal05}, $8$ \cite{Via17} and $24$ \cite{CKM17}
  only.}

Let $\mathcal{C}\subset\mathbb{R}^4$ be the centre set of a packing of
unit balls, so that $|c-c'|\ge2$ for distinct $c,c'\in\mathcal{C}$, and
let $c\in\mathcal{C}$. The Voronoi cell
\[
  V_c=\bigl\{z\in\mathbb{R}^4 : |z-c|\le|z-c'| \text{ for all } c'\in\mathcal{C}\bigr\}
\]
of a point of the $D_4$ lattice is a regular $24$-cell circumscribed about
the unit ball, of volume $8$. The \emph{twenty-four-cell conjecture}
asserts that every Voronoi cell of every unit-ball packing of
$\mathbb{R}^4$ has volume at least $8$. It implies $\Delta_4=\pi^2/16$,
because the cells tile space and each contains one ball
(\cref{sec:density-derivation}).\footnote{In a periodic packing with
  period lattice $L$ and $N$ centres in a fundamental domain, the cells of
  one period tile a fundamental domain, so their volumes add up to $\det L$
  and the density is $N\vol(B^4)/\det L$, with $\vol(B^4)=\pi^2/2$. A lower
  bound $v$ for every cell therefore bounds the density by $\vol(B^4)/v$,
  and $v=8$ gives $\pi^2/16$. A general packing can be enlarged to a
  saturated one, in which every cell lies within distance $2$ of its centre,
  and the same count over a large ball gives the same bound
  (\cref{lem:cells-density}). The implication runs one way only: a density
  bound says nothing about an individual cell.} In dimension three the
corresponding statement, with the rhombic dodecahedron in place of the
$24$-cell, is false (\cref{sec:history-new}). In dimension four it is
open; Musin \cite{Mus18} discusses it and proposes an approach through the
contact graph on $S^3$.

This paper proves the conjecture at every centre whose neighbours within
$2\sqrt2$ touch it, at every centre with at most twenty-three or at least
twenty-nine other centres within $\sqrt6$, and in an explicit neighbourhood
of the $D_4$ configuration, and it reduces the remaining centres to two
explicit statements about at most twenty-eight neighbours; the cases left open
are those of \cref{conj:shell} below.

\subsection{Results}\label{sec:results}

Two radii govern which neighbours matter. A centre at distance $d$ from
$c$ cuts $V_c$ only outside the ball of radius $d/2$ about $c$, so centres
at distance $2\sqrt2$ or more never cut the $24$-cell that a $D_4$
configuration about $c$ would give (\cref{lem:shell-new}); and the centres
whose half-spaces meet the ball $B(c,\sqrt{3/2})$ are exactly those within
$\sqrt6$ of $c$ (\cref{fig:radii}). A \emph{contact} of $c$ is a centre at
distance exactly $2$, and $\Rt$ denotes the $D_4$ root system, normalised so that its
$24$ roots have length $\sqrt2$.

\begin{thm}[General local bound]\label{thm:local-general}
Let $V_c$ be the Voronoi cell of a centre $c$ of a unit-ball packing of
$\mathbb{R}^4$, and let $d_1\le d_2\le\dots$ be the distances from $c$ of
the other centres closer than $2\sqrt2$. If every $d_i$ equals $2$, or if
$\Phi(d_1,d_2,\dots)>8$ for the function $\Phi$ of
\cref{prop:distance-criterion}, then $\vol(V_c)\ge8$, with equality if and
only if the centres within $2\sqrt2$ of $c$ form a copy of $\sqrt2\,\Rt$
about $c$.
\end{thm}

When every neighbour within $2\sqrt2$ is a contact, the theorem is the
twenty-four-cell conjecture for contact configurations, and its proof
divides by the number $m$ of contacts, which is at most $24$ by Musin's
theorem \cite{Mus08}. For $m\le22$ a covering estimate on $S^3$ gives
$\vol(V_c)\ge8.0463\ldots$ whatever the directions
(\cref{thm:local-fewcontacts}). For $m=24$ the contacts form a copy of
$\sqrt2\,\Rt$ and $V_c$ is the $24$-cell; this is the classification of
twenty-four-point kissing configurations, due to de Laat, Leijenhorst and
de Muinck Keizer \cite{LLM24} and proved here from their positive kernel,
whose properties we verify in exact arithmetic
(\cref{thm:m24,thm:twentyfour-points}).\footnote{The classification
  states that any $24$ unit vectors in $\mathbb{R}^4$ with pairwise inner
  products at most $\frac12$ form a copy of the $D_4$ root system. Its
  proof has two halves. The first is a semidefinite bound: the second level
  of the hierarchy for spherical codes in the form of de Laat and Vallentin
  \cite{dLV15} bounds the size of a code by the value $K(\emptyset,\emptyset)$
  of any positive kernel $K$ on the subsets of at most two points of the
  sphere that satisfies a family of linear inequalities. The authors of
  \cite{LLM24} found such a kernel with value exactly $24$, at truncation
  degrees $14$ and $16$, reduced by the symmetry of the sphere and solved to
  forty digits, and rounded it to an exact rational point by the procedure
  of \cite{CdLL24}; that point is public \cite{LLM24data}, under a licence
  that permits redistribution. Because the bound is exactly $24$ and a code
  of size $24$ exists, the equality case forces every inner product of such
  a code into the zero set of the two-point polynomial of the kernel, which
  is $\{-1,-\frac12,0,\frac12\}$. Both the bound and its equality case are
  proved in the text (\cref{lem:las2}), the positivity of the kernel is
  proved from the form in which it is built (\cref{lem:gram}), and every
  property of the rational point that these lemmas use is verified in exact
  arithmetic (\cref{prop:verified}): the format of the data, the positive
  definiteness of all $127$ blocks, the construction of the zonal matrices,
  the four polynomial identities, the value $24$ and the zeros of the
  two-point polynomial, the last also in Lean. The second half is
  classical. Scaled by $\sqrt2$ the vectors have norm $2$ and integer inner
  products, so they lie among the norm-$2$ vectors of an integral lattice
  of rank at most $4$; these form a simply laced root system, and among the
  root systems of rank at most $4$ only $D_4$ has $24$ roots
  (\cref{lem:root-lattice}). The earlier bounds on the same question are the
  three-point bound of Bachoc and Vallentin \cite{BV08}, whose value in
  dimension four, $24.5797$ in \cite{BV08}, has been lowered to $24.0569$
  at degree $16$ \cite{MV10,MO18} and to $24.0472$ at degree $20$
  \cite{Lei25}, and Musin's theorem \cite{Mus08}, which bounds the size of
  the code but does not identify it.} The case $m=23$ is neither small
enough for a covering estimate nor rigid enough for a classification. We
prove it by an identity: inside the ball of radius $\sqrt{3/2}$ no three
contact caps meet, so the volume of the cell there is an exact function
of the pair angles, and the bound becomes an inequality between pair
angles that a three-point semidefinite certificate proves
(\cref{thm:m23,thm:strict-reduction,thm:certificate}).

\begin{thm}[Further cases of the local bound]\label{thm:intro-closure}
Let $c$ be a centre of a unit-ball packing of $\mathbb{R}^4$ and $Y$ the
set of other centres within $\sqrt6$ of $c$. The conclusion of
\cref{thm:local-general} holds at $c$ in each of the following cases:
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\alph*)},leftmargin=2.2em]
\item $|Y|\le23$, at any distances;
\item $|Y|\ge31$, and then $\vol(V_c)>8.0179$;
\item $|Y|=29$ or $|Y|=30$;
\item $|Y|=24$, and $Y$ lies on the rays from $c$ through the roots of a
copy of $\Rt$;
\item the contact directions of $c$ contain all but at most two of the
normalised roots of a copy of $\Rt$, or more generally one of the three
orthogonal pairs of normalised roots at each vertex of its $24$-cell;
\item no centre lies at a distance from $c$ strictly between
$2+\varepsilon_0$ and $\sqrt6$, where $\varepsilon_0=4\cdot10^{-26}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{thm}

These are \cref{thm:m23-labelled,thm:count31,thm:count30,thm:count29,cor:rays,cor:root-subsets,thm:near-contact}.
Case (f) makes the $D_4$ configuration a strict local minimum of the cell
volume among all packings, in an explicit neighbourhood; the constant
$\varepsilon_0$ comes from a robust form of the classification
(\cref{thm:m24-robust}). Cases (a), (b) and (c) rest on semidefinite
certificates whose kernels carry the distance of each centre from $c$ as a
label, and their verification is exact or in interval arithmetic.

What \cref{thm:local-general,thm:intro-closure} leave is the following.

\begin{conjecture}[Crowded near-contacts]\label{conj:shell}
The conclusion of \cref{thm:local-general} holds at every centre of every
unit-ball packing of $\mathbb{R}^4$, also when some centre lies at a
distance strictly between $2$ and $2\sqrt2$ from it and
$\Phi(d_1,d_2,\dots)\le8$.
\end{conjecture}

The twenty-four-cell conjecture is \cref{thm:local-general} together with
\cref{conj:shell}. By \cref{thm:intro-closure} and
\cref{thm:kissing-stable}, a centre at which \cref{conj:shell} fails has
between $24$ and $28$ other centres within $\sqrt6$, one of them at a
distance between $2+\varepsilon_0$ and $\sqrt6$, and at most $24$ within
$2.0161$. For a set $Y$ of centres within $\sqrt6$ of $c$ let $T(Y)$ be the
volume of the part of $B(c,\sqrt{3/2})$ that no half-space of $Y$ cuts off,
and $K(Y)$ the convex hull of $c$ and the images of the centres of $Y$ under
inversion in the sphere of radius $2$ about $c$.
\Cref{prop:reduction-G} shows that \cref{conj:shell} follows from two
statements:
\begin{enumerate}[leftmargin=2.6em]
\item[\textup{(G)}] if $|Y|=24$ and $T(Y)\le8$, then the cell $V(Y)$ that
$Y$ alone cuts out satisfies $\vol(V(Y)\cap K(Y))\ge8$, with equality only
for a copy of $\sqrt2\,\Rt$;
\item[\textup{(C)}] if $|Y|\ge25$, then $T(Y)>8$.
\end{enumerate}
We prove (C) for $|Y|\ge29$ (\cref{thm:count31,thm:count30,thm:count29}), at
thirty and twenty-nine with a kernel on triples of directions that carry the
range of their distance from $c$ as a type; for $25\le|Y|\le28$ we prove lower bounds on
the number of centres within $2.05$, $2.1$, $2.15$ and $2.2$
(\cref{prop:C-radial}). We prove (G) along
every push-out of the root system and in a neighbourhood of it
(\cref{thm:G-pushout,thm:near-contact}). Statement (G) in general, and (C)
for $25\le|Y|\le28$, are open, and \cref{sec:G-and-C} sets out what each
needs; \cref{fig:proof-map} shows the cases. The density bound does not
require them cell by cell:
\cref{lem:redistribution} shows that it suffices to move volume between
neighbouring cells, antisymmetrically, so that every centre ends with at
least $8$, and the theorems above say that no transfer is needed at the
centres they cover. Whether such a transfer exists is open as well.

\begin{figure}[tb]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.94\textwidth]{fig_tikz_proofmap}
\caption{The cases of the local bound $\vol(V_c)\ge8$. Left, contact
configurations, by the number $m$ of contacts:
\cref{cor:m22,thm:m23,thm:m24}. Right, a centre within $\sqrt6$ of $c$
that is not a contact, by the number $|Y|$ of centres within $\sqrt6$:
\cref{thm:m23-labelled,thm:count31,thm:count30,thm:count29,lem:no-triple}, statement
(C) of \cref{prop:reduction-G} with the counts of \cref{prop:C-radial},
and statement (G) with \cref{thm:G-pushout,thm:near-contact}. Green cases
are proved and orange cases are open.}
\label{fig:proof-map}
\end{figure}

\begin{thm}[Density bound]\label{thm:main-general}\label{cor:density-uncond}
Let $\mathcal{C}$ be the centre set of a unit-ball packing of
$\mathbb{R}^4$ that is periodic or saturated, and suppose that every centre
of $\mathcal{C}$ satisfies the hypothesis of \cref{thm:local-general} or one
of the conditions of \cref{thm:intro-closure}. Then the density of the
packing is at most $\pi^2/16$, with equality for the $D_4$ lattice at
nearest-neighbour distance $2$.
\end{thm}

Without the hypothesis on the centres the same cell bounds still control
the density. At a centre with $24$ to $28$ others within $\sqrt6$ the cell
is not shown to reach $8$, but it is shown to exceed $7.7681$, and this gives
the following bound for every packing (\cref{sec:density-cells}).

\begin{thm}[Density from the cells]\label{thm:intro-density}
Every packing of unit balls in $\mathbb{R}^4$ has density at most
$0.63527$.
\end{thm}

This is below the three-point bound $0.63611$ of Cohn, de Laat and Salmon
\cite{CdLS22} and the linear programming bound $0.64774$ of Cohn and Elkies
\cite{CE03}, and it uses nothing but the volume of each cell inside the
ball of radius $\sqrt{3/2}$ about its centre.

\subsection{Methods}\label{sec:methods-intro}

The single-deviation case has a proof that uses no certificate at all,
and it is the model for the others. Call the centres within $2\sqrt2$ of
$c$ its \emph{active neighbours}, and write
$V_c^{\mathrm{act}}\supseteq V_c$ for the cell that they alone cut out.

\begin{thm}[Local cell bound, single deviation]\label{thm:local-uncond}
Let $c$ be a centre of a unit-ball packing of $\mathbb{R}^4$ whose active
neighbours lie in root directions of a copy of $\Rt$ except for at most
one, and, if there is one, suppose that some root direction of $\Rt$ is
taken by no active neighbour. Then $\vol(V_c^{\mathrm{act}})\ge8$, with
equality if and only if the active neighbours of $c$ form a copy of
$\sqrt2\,\Rt$ centred at $c$. Nothing is assumed about the deviating
direction or about the size of its deviation.
\end{thm}

Fix the deviating contact direction and let $Q$ be the intersection of
the half-spaces of the twenty-three contact directions that remain at
roots. The cell at the all-contact corner is $Q$ cut by one further
half-space at unit distance from the centre, and $\vol(Q)=\frac{25}{3}$;
so the cell has volume at least $8$ exactly when that half-space cuts at
most $\frac13$ off $Q$ (\cref{prop:cap-reformulation}). The sixteen roots
at $60^\circ$ and $120^\circ$ to the deviating root cut out the
cross-polytope
\[
  B=\bigl\{x : |x_0|+|x_1|+|x_2|+|x_3|\le2\bigr\}
\]
in the frame of four pairwise orthogonal roots (\cref{lem:crosspolytope}),
so $Q\subseteq B$, and the enclosure loses nothing in the one direction
that matters, since $\{x_0\ge1\}$ cuts exactly $\frac13$ off both. What
remains is a question about the cross-polytope alone, answered by
\cref{thm:cap-inequality}: among the half-spaces at unit distance from the
centre, those normal to a vertex direction cut off the most. Its proof,
by hand, writes the cap volume as a divided difference of
$a(2\sqrt a-1)_+^4$, that is, as an average of the third derivative of
this function over a simplex, and uses that the third derivative is
nondecreasing and, beyond $\frac14$, concave (\cref{fig:cap-scheme}).

The covering estimate measures the cell radially. With $\delta(\theta)$ the
angular distance from $\theta\in S^3$ to the nearest contact direction, the
cell at the all-contact corner has volume
$\frac14\int_{S^3}\sec^4\delta$ (\cref{lem:radial-form}). The contacts are
$60^\circ$ apart, so caps of radius $r$ about them leave at least
$2\pi^2-m\pi(2r-\sin2r)$ of the sphere uncovered, and integrating gives
$\vol(V_c)\ge\frac{\pi m}{3}\tan^3r_m$ with $2r_m-\sin2r_m=2\pi/m$.

\begin{thm}[Local cell bound, few contacts]\label{thm:local-fewcontacts}
Let $c$ be a centre of a unit-ball packing of $\mathbb{R}^4$ every active
neighbour of which is a contact, and suppose there are at most $22$ of
them. Then $\vol(V_c)\ge8.0463\ldots>8$, whatever their directions. With
no bound on their number the same argument gives $\vol(V_c)\ge7.7989\ldots$.
\end{thm}

With several deviating directions and twenty-four contacts, an exact
free-volume identity (\cref{thm:freevol-new}) turns the bound into a
comparison of two unions of pieces of the cell.

\begin{thm}[Multi-direction positivity]\label{conj:multidir-new}
Let the configuration be at the all-contact corner with a full active
set, and let $D$ be any set of $m\ge2$ roots whose directions are
deviated, with arbitrary tilts and arbitrary deviation directions. Then,
in the notation of \cref{thm:freevol-new},
\[
  \vol\Bigl(\bigcup_{j\in D}E_j\Bigr)
  \;\ge\;
  \vol\Bigl(\bigcup_{j\in D}C_j\Bigr).
\]
\end{thm}

It follows from the classification, since twenty-four contacts admit no
deviation (\cref{cor:conj-resolved}). The corresponding statement with sums
of volumes in place of volumes of unions is a different assertion, which
neither implies it nor follows from it (\cref{rem:sums-not-unions}); an
additivity argument of this kind in the preprint \cite{BBB26} of the first,
second and fourth authors is not correct as stated there and is not used
here.

Beyond contacts, the cell is measured inside $B(c,\sqrt{3/2})$. There no
three half-spaces of the centres within $\sqrt6$ cut a common point
(\cref{lem:no-triple}), so $\vol(V_c)\ge T(Y)$, and $T(Y)$ is
$\frac98\pi^2$ less the caps of the centres plus their pairwise
overlaps, an explicit function of the distances $|y|$ and of the angles
between the directions. A two-point or three-point kernel that carries the
distance from $c$ as a label, continuous or by ranges, then bounds $T(Y)$
from below over all configurations at once (\cref{sec:kernels}). That is how
\cref{thm:m23-labelled,thm:count31,thm:count30,thm:count29} are proved; the same
kernels without the label count the centres near $c$
(\cref{thm:kissing-stable,thm:twenty-six}). For twenty-four centres $T$ can fall below $8$, and the cell has to
be bounded through the hull $K(Y)$ (\cref{prop:inversion-hull}); this is
where (G) enters.

\subsection{Organisation}\label{sec:argument-structure}

\Cref{sec:root-system} fixes the root system, the $24$-cell and three
reductions: shell localisation, the root-aligned case, and monotonicity in
the contact radii. \Cref{sec:density-derivation} passes from cells to
density. \Cref{sec:kernels} proves the positivity theorems on which every
certificate of the paper rests, the general form in which a positive
kernel bounds a sum over a configuration, and the lemmas that make a
finite check of a certificate into a proof; it also lists the
certificates. \Cref{sec:deviation-domain} proves the free-volume identity
and the covering bound, settles twenty-four contacts by the
classification, and proves the case of twenty-three contacts.
\Cref{sec:cap-theorem} proves \cref{thm:local-uncond}. \Cref{sec:noncontact}
treats neighbours that do not touch: the distance criterion, the cases of
\cref{thm:intro-closure}, the second order of the volume at the root
system, and the statements (G) and (C). \Cref{sec:density-cells} proves
\cref{thm:intro-density}. \Cref{sec:comparison-methods} compares the
method with others, \cref{sec:status} summarises what is proved and what
is open, and \cref{sec:reproducibility-new} describes how the computations
are checked. Two appendices follow the cell further than the theorems
need: \cref{app:single-deviation} computes the defect of a single
deviation region by region, and \cref{app:several-deviations} studies
several deviating contacts to second order and along exact paths. No
proof in the main text uses them.
\Cref{fig:corner} shows the objects of the argument in the
three-dimensional analogue, where they can be drawn: the face-centred
cubic lattice, the rhombic dodecahedron and its twelve contacts.

\begin{figure}[tb]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.78\textwidth]{fig_c_corner}
\caption{The three-dimensional analogue. (a) The all-contact corner: the
unit ball about the centre (blue), its twelve contact points (clay), and the
twelve unit balls that touch it, at distance $2$ in the contact directions
of the face-centred cubic lattice. (b) The Voronoi cell of the centre, the
rhombic dodecahedron, with one facet at distance $1$ for each contact and
volume $4\sqrt2=5.6569$. (c) Monotonicity in the radii: one neighbour moves
from distance $2$ out to $2.6$, its half-space recedes, and the cell grows
from $5.6569$ to $5.9666$; the ochre solid is the volume gained. (d) A
deviation: one contact direction is tilted by $25^\circ$ at distance $2$;
the cell gains the teal volume and loses the clay volume, and rises from
$5.6569$ to $5.7470$. The volumes are computed from the half-space
intersections.}
\label{fig:corner}
\end{figure}

\subsection{Background}\label{sec:history-new}

In the plane, Thue \cite{Thu10} announced the hexagonal bound and Fejes
T\'oth \cite{FT42} gave the first complete proof,
$\Delta_2=\pi/\sqrt{12}$. In three dimensions Kepler's assertion that the
face-centred cubic arrangement is optimal was proved by Hales, through the
programme begun in \cite{Hal97} and completed in \cite{Hal05}, and then in
a machine-checked form \cite{Hal17}. There the local statement that plays
the part of the twenty-four-cell conjecture is false: a Voronoi cell of a
packing of $\mathbb{R}^3$ can be smaller than the rhombic dodecahedron,
and the least is the regular dodecahedron, as Fejes T\'oth conjectured and
Hales and McLaughlin proved \cite{HM10}.\footnote{The regular dodecahedron
  circumscribed about a unit ball has volume $5.5503\ldots$, against
  $4\sqrt2=5.6568\ldots$ for the rhombic dodecahedron; it occurs as a
  Voronoi cell when twelve balls touch the central one at the vertices of
  an icosahedron, a configuration that does not extend to a dense packing.
  This is why Hales's proof uses a hybrid of Voronoi cells and Delaunay
  simplices rather than Voronoi cells alone.} The lattice case is older and
easier. Korkine and Zolotareff \cite{KZ1872} showed that the quaternary form
of $D_4$ has the least determinant among positive quaternary forms of
given minimum; Voronoi's reduction theory \cite{Vor08}, whose polyhedral
language we use, placed such results on a systematic footing, and Martinet
\cite{Mar03} gives the modern account. Upper bounds for all packings begin
with Blichfeldt's method \cite{Bli29}, which gives
$\Delta_4\le\frac34$, and were sharpened by the simplex bound of Rogers
\cite{Rog64}. Gruber and Lekkerkerker \cite{Gru87} survey the classical
theory, Conway and Sloane \cite{CS1988} is the standard source for $D_4$
and its Voronoi cell, and Bezdek \cite{Bez10} collects the local and
combinatorial side of the subject.

Cohn and Elkies \cite{CE03} recast the upper bound as a linear programme
over radial Schwartz functions whose values and Fourier transforms obey
sign conditions; equality requires a single function with a prescribed
sequence of zeros. Viazovska \cite{Via17} constructed that function in
dimension $8$ from quasimodular forms, and the Leech case followed
\cite{CKM17}. In dimension $4$ the programme cannot be sharp: Li
\cite{Li25} bounds its optimum from below by a finite-dimensional
reduction, and in dimension $4$ that lower bound is a centre density of
$0.12914461$ against the $0.125$ of $D_4$.\footnote{The centre density is
  the density divided by $\vol(B^4)=\pi^2/2$, so the centre density of
  $D_4$ at distance $2$ is $\frac{1}{8}$. Cohn and Elkies \cite{CE03}
  report an upper bound of $0.13126$ for it; the gap between $0.12914$ and
  $0.125$ is a property of the programme, not of the functions tried.} The
three-point bound of Cohn, de Laat and Salmon \cite{CdLS22}, the best upper
bound known in dimension four, and the refinements of Cohn and Zhao
\cite{CZ14}, which couple the packing bound to spherical-code bounds, also
stay above $\pi^2/16$.

The kissing problem, which asks only how many unit balls can touch one,
was settled in dimension four by Musin \cite{Mus08}, who showed that the
answer is $24$ by adding a polynomial constraint to the linear programming
bound; the three-dimensional statement is the theorem of Sch\"utte and van
der Waerden \cite{SW52}. A kissing bound constrains the contacts at one
centre and says nothing about volume, so it does not imply the packing
bound; it is the first ingredient of \cref{thm:local-general}. The route
taken here is local. It uses the symmetry of $D_4$, the Weyl group of order
$192$ and the self-duality of the $24$-cell, with the polyhedral facts of
\cite{Zie95,Gru03} and the root-system conventions of \cite{Hum90}, and no
Fourier analysis. Semidefinite programming enters only as a search for
certificates, which are then checked in exact rational arithmetic, in
interval arithmetic and, where the text says so, in the Lean proof
assistant.

\section{The \texorpdfstring{$D_4$}{D4} root system and its Voronoi cell}\label{sec:root-system}

\subsection{Lattice and roots}

Write $D_4=\{m\in\mathbb{Z}^4 : m_1+m_2+m_3+m_4\equiv0\pmod2\}$ for the
standard integral $D_4$ lattice, and set $\Latt=\sqrt2\,D_4$, which has
nearest-neighbour distance $2$, the natural normalisation for a
unit-ball packing. The associated root system is
\[
  \Rt \;=\; \{\pm e_i \pm e_j : 1\le i<j\le4\}, \qquad
  |\Rt|=24, \qquad |\alpha|^2=2 \ \text{ for every } \alpha\in\Rt.
\]
The Weyl group $W(D_4)$ acts on $\Rt$ by signed permutations of the
coordinates with an even number of sign changes; it has order $192$ and
acts transitively on $\Rt$.

\begin{lem}[Gram values on $\Rt$]\label{lem:gram-new}
For distinct $\alpha,\beta\in\Rt$, the normalised inner product
$s_{\alpha\beta}=\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle/2$ takes one of the four
values $-1,-\tfrac12,0,\tfrac12$, and $s_{\alpha\beta}=-1$ occurs only
for $\beta=-\alpha$. The three remaining values correspond respectively
to $\alpha-\beta\in\Rt$ (for $s=\tfrac12$), $\alpha\perp\beta$ (for
$s=0$), and $\alpha+\beta\in\Rt$ (for $s=-\tfrac12$).
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
Since $\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{Z}^4$, $\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle\in\mathbb{Z}$,
and Cauchy--Schwarz gives $|\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle|\le|\alpha||\beta|=2$
with equality forcing $\beta=\pm\alpha$. For the remaining integer values
$\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle\in\{-1,0,1\}$, expand
$|\alpha\mp\beta|^2=|\alpha|^2+|\beta|^2\mp2\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle
=4\mp2\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle\in\{2,4,6\}$. An integer vector of squared
length $2$ has two entries $\pm1$ and the others $0$, so it lies in $\Rt$;
hence $\alpha-\beta\in\Rt$ when $\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=1$ and
$\alpha+\beta\in\Rt$ when $\langle\alpha,\beta\rangle=-1$.
\end{proof}

The four values $-1$ (antipodal pairs), $\tfrac12$ (acute pairs), $0$
(orthogonal pairs) and $-\tfrac12$ (obtuse pairs) enter every computation
at the all-contact corner below; the Hessian of \cref{prop:hessian-d4} and
the polytope $Q$ of \cref{sec:cap-theorem} are built from them.

\subsection{The reference cell}

\begin{definition}\label{def:refcell-new}
The \emph{reference cell} is
\[
  \VCell \;=\; \bigl\{z\in\mathbb{R}^4 : \langle z,\alpha\rangle\le\sqrt2
  \ \text{for all}\ \alpha\in\Rt\bigr\}.
\]
\end{definition}

\begin{lem}\label{lem:refcell-vol}
$\VCell$ has exactly $24$ vertices, all of norm $\sqrt2$:
\[
  \mathrm{vert}(\VCell) \;=\;
  \{\pm\sqrt2\,e_i : 1\le i\le4\} \;\cup\;
  \Bigl\{\tfrac{1}{\sqrt2}(\epsilon_1,\epsilon_2,\epsilon_3,\epsilon_4) :
  \epsilon_i\in\{\pm1\}\Bigr\},
\]
an $8$-point axis family and a $16$-point sign family. $\vol(\VCell)=8$,
and the symmetry group of $\VCell$, the Weyl group $W(F_4)$ of order
$1152$, contains $W(D_4)$ as a normal subgroup of index $6$, the quotient
being the group $S_3$ of automorphisms of the Dynkin diagram of $D_4$.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
Since $W(D_4)$ is transitive on $\Rt$, it suffices to describe the facet
$F=\VCell\cap\{z_1+z_2=\sqrt2\}$. On $F$ the constraint $|z_1|+|z_2|\le\sqrt2$
forces $z_1,z_2\ge0$; write $z_1=\frac{1+a}{\sqrt2}$, $z_2=\frac{1-a}{\sqrt2}$
and $z_j=\frac{b_j}{\sqrt2}$ for $j=3,4$. The constraints
$|z_i|+|z_j|\le\sqrt2$ with $i\in\{1,2\}$, $j\in\{3,4\}$ become
$|a|+|b_j|\le1$, and the constraint for $\{3,4\}$ follows from these. So $F$
is the square bipyramid $|a|+\max(|b_3|,|b_4|)\le1$, whose vertices
$(a,b_3,b_4)=(\pm1,0,0)$ and $(0,\pm1,\pm1)$ are the points $\sqrt2\,e_1$,
$\sqrt2\,e_2$ and $\frac1{\sqrt2}(1,1,\pm1,\pm1)$. A unit step in $a$ has length
$1$ in $\mathbb{R}^4$ and a unit step in $b_j$ has length $\frac1{\sqrt2}$,
so every edge of $F$ has length $\sqrt2$: $F$ is a regular octahedron of edge
$\sqrt2$ and volume $\frac43$. Every vertex of $\VCell$ is a vertex of a
facet, which gives the $24$ listed points. The facet hyperplanes lie at
distance $1$ from $0$, so $\VCell$ is the union of $24$ pyramids of height
$1$ over its facets, each of volume $\frac14\cdot\frac43=\frac13$, and
$\vol(\VCell)=8$. The symmetry group of the regular $24$-cell is $W(F_4)$,
of order $1152$ \cite[\S 8.2]{Cox73}. It permutes the three families of
eight vertices (the axis family and the two halves of the sign family split
by the parity of the number of minus signs) through all of $S_3$, and the
subgroup fixing each family is $W(D_4)$, of order $192$.
\end{proof}

The $24$-cell is the Voronoi cell of $D_4$ \cite[Ch.~VIII]{Cox73},
\cite[Ch.~4]{CS1988}.%
\footnote{Since the $24$-cell is the one object every later section
  perturbs, its arithmetic is collected here. Take $\Rt$ to
  be the $24$ integer vectors $\pm e_i\pm e_j$, $i<j$, each of squared
  length $2$; the lattice $D_4$ they generate is
  $\{x\in\mathbb{Z}^4:\sum x_i\ \text{even}\}$, of covolume $2$, with
  minimum $\sqrt2$ and kissing number $24$. Scaling by $\sqrt2$ puts the
  nearest centres at distance $2$, as a unit-ball packing requires, and
  the covolume of $\Latt=\sqrt2\,D_4$ is $4\cdot2=8$, which is the volume of one Voronoi
  cell and the number $8$ that every bound in this paper is measured
  against. The cell itself, $\VCell=\{x:\langle
  x,\alpha\rangle\le1\ \text{for all}\ \alpha\in\Rt/\sqrt2\}$, is the
  regular $24$-cell: $24$ vertices, $96$ edges, $96$ triangular faces,
  $24$ octahedral facets, Schl\"afli symbol $\{3,4,3\}$, self-dual, and
  with each facet centred on one of the $24$ contact directions. Its
  vertices split under $W(D_4)$ into the $8$ points $\pm\sqrt2\,e_i$ and
  the $16$ points $(\pm1,\pm1,\pm1,\pm1)/\sqrt2$, which is the axis and
  sign division used throughout. Two numbers recur: the support function
  of $\VCell$ equals $1$ at every root direction, which is
  \cref{lem:root-aligned-new} in one line; and the circumradius is
  $\sqrt2$, so the inradius-to-circumradius ratio is $1/\sqrt2$, and a
  centre at distance $2\sqrt2$ or more cannot cut the cell
  (\cref{lem:shell-new}). The density $\pi^2/16$ is
  $\vol(B^4)/8=(\pi^2/2)/8$.}

\Cref{fig:cell24} draws the polytope from its coordinates. Panel (a) is an
orthogonal projection to $\mathbb{R}^3$, in which the axis family and
the sign family are told apart by colour, (b) is the
Schlegel diagram from one octahedral facet, (c) is the
three-dimensional form of the cap inequality of \cref{sec:cap-theorem}, and
(d) is the decomposition into the three $16$-cells that
triality permutes.

\begin{figure}[tb]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{fig_c_cell24}
\caption{(a) The reference cell, the regular $24$-cell $\VCell$, in an orthogonal projection to three dimensions: the $8$ vertices of the axis family (teal), the $16$ of the sign family (blue), the $32$ edges inside the sign family and the $64$ joining the two families (grey). (b) The Schlegel diagram of the same cell from one octahedral facet (clay): the remaining $18$ vertices and $84$ edges fill the interior of that facet with the other $23$ octahedra. (c) The cap inequality one dimension down. The octahedron of inradius $1$ carries its inscribed ball; the half-space at unit distance normal to a vertex cuts off the clay pyramid, drawn displaced along its normal, and the one normal to a facet touches that facet (teal) and cuts off nothing. (d) The same $24$ vertices read as three $16$-cells, which triality permutes, drawn side by side in one orthogonal projection, each with its own $24$ edges and the hull of its image: the axis family (teal) and the sign family split by the parity of the number of minus signs (ochre, violet).}
\label{fig:cell24}
\end{figure}

\begin{definition}[Support function and angular gap]\label{def:supp-new}
For $u\in S^3$, the support function of $\VCell$ is
$h(u)=\max_{z\in\VCell}\langle z,u\rangle$, and the \emph{angular gap}
is $\gamma(u)=h(u)-1$.
\end{definition}

\begin{lem}\label{lem:hmin-new}
For every $u\in S^3$, $h(u)\ge1$, with equality exactly on the $24$
unit root directions $\alpha/\sqrt2$, $\alpha\in\Rt$. The maximum
$h(u)=\sqrt2$ is attained exactly at the $24$ vertex directions
$v/\sqrt2$, $v$ a vertex of $\VCell$, so $\gamma(u)\in[0,\sqrt2-1]$
throughout, vanishing exactly on root directions.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
For $u\in S^3$ and $\alpha\in\Rt$, $\langle u,\alpha\rangle\le|\alpha|=\sqrt2$,
so $u\in\VCell$ and $h(u)\ge\langle u,u\rangle=1$. If $h(u)=1$, the point
$u$ maximises $\langle z,u\rangle$ over $\VCell$ and lies on its boundary,
so $\langle u,\alpha\rangle=\sqrt2$ for some $\alpha$, and equality in the
Cauchy--Schwarz inequality gives $u=\alpha/\sqrt2$. Conversely
$h(\alpha/\sqrt2)\le1$ by the definition of $\VCell$. For the upper bound,
$h(u)=\max_v\langle v,u\rangle\le\max_v|v|=\sqrt2$ over the vertices $v$ of
\cref{lem:refcell-vol}, with equality exactly when $u=v/|v|$ for a vertex
$v$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Conventions used throughout}

We fix, once and for all, the following conventions.

\begin{notation}\label{not:conventions-new}
A \emph{packing centre} is a point $c$ in the centre set $\mathcal{C}$
of a unit-ball packing; its \emph{active neighbours} are those centres
$y\in\mathcal{C}$ with $|y-c|<2\sqrt2$ (\cref{sec:shell-new} shows this
is the only range that can matter). An active neighbour's \emph{contact
direction} is $u=(y-c)/|y-c|\in S^3$. The \emph{all-contact corner} is
the configuration in which every active radius equals the minimum
packing radius $2$ exactly. The normalised roots are
$\base_i=\alpha_i/\sqrt2$. A deviating contact direction is written
$\base_1=\cos\tilt\,\base_0+\sin\tilt\,\dev$, where $\base_0$ is the root
it deviates from, $\tilt$ the tilt angle and $\dev$ a unit vector
orthogonal to $\base_0$, the deviation direction; the volume defect
$\vol(V_c)-8$ of such a configuration is written $\defect(\tilt,\dev)$.
\end{notation}

\Cref{app:notation} collects every piece of notation used more than
once in this paper, symbol by symbol, for reference.

\subsection{Shell localisation}\label{sec:shell-new}

\begin{lem}[Shell localisation]\label{lem:shell-new}
Let $c$ be a centre of a unit-ball packing and $R>0$. A centre $y$ with
$|y-c|\ge2R$ does not cut the ball $B(c,R)$: its half-space
$\{z:|z-c|\le|z-y|\}$ contains $B(c,R)$. In particular the centres at
distance at least $2\sqrt2$ from $c$ contain $B(c,\sqrt2)$, and with it
the reference cell $c+\VCell$, in their half-spaces; and if no other
centre lies within $2\sqrt2$ of $c$, then $V_c\supseteq c+\VCell$ and
$\vol(V_c)\ge8$.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
The half-space of $y$ is $\{z:\langle z-c,u\rangle\le|y-c|/2\}$, with $u$
the unit vector from $c$ towards $y$, and $|y-c|/2\ge R$. The reference
cell has circumradius $\sqrt2$ (\cref{lem:refcell-vol}).
\end{proof}

\begin{figure}[tb]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.9\textwidth]{fig_tikz_radii}
\caption{The radii that decide which centres cut $V_c$, in a plane through
$c$, to scale. A centre $y$ cuts $V_c$ along the bisector
$\langle x,y/|y|\rangle=|y|/2$. A contact, $|y|=2$, gives a facet tangent to
the unit ball; a centre with $2<|y|<\sqrt6$ cuts a cap off the ball
$B(\sqrt{3/2})$, which is where \cref{lem:no-triple} measures the cell; at
$|y|=\sqrt6$ the bisector is tangent to $B(\sqrt{3/2})$, and at
$|y|=2\sqrt2$ to $B(\sqrt2)$, the ball circumscribed about the $24$-cell
(\cref{lem:shell-new}).}
\label{fig:radii}
\end{figure}

The lemma does not say that only the centres at distance in
$[2,2\sqrt2)$ can affect $\vol(V_c)$. That is true of the part of
$V_c$ inside $B(c,\sqrt2)$ and false outside it. A cell that
reaches beyond $\sqrt2$, as the cell of the deletion of a root does, its
circumradius being $2$, can be cut there by a centre farther than
$2\sqrt2$: with the twenty-three roots of a deletion at distance $2$ and
one more centre at distance $2.9$ on the axis of the deleted root, which
is a packing, the cell loses $\tfrac13(2-1.45)^4$ and has volume
$\tfrac{1328453}{160000}=8.3028\ldots$ instead of $\tfrac{25}3$. Every argument below that bounds the cell
of a packing either stays inside $B(c,\sqrt2)$ or says what it assumes
about the centres beyond $2\sqrt2$.

\subsection{The root-aligned case}\label{sec:root-aligned-new}

\begin{lem}[Root-aligned configurations]\label{lem:root-aligned-new}
If every active neighbour direction $u$ (in the sense of
\cref{not:conventions-new}) is an exact root direction
$\alpha/\sqrt2$, $\alpha\in\Rt$, then $\vol(V_c)\ge8$.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
Take $c=0$. An active neighbour at distance $d\ge2$ in the root
direction $\alpha/\sqrt2$ contributes the half-space
$\{z:\langle z,\alpha/\sqrt2\rangle\le d/2\}$, which contains
$\{z:\langle z,\alpha/\sqrt2\rangle\le1\}$ and hence $\VCell$, since
$h(\alpha/\sqrt2)=1$ by \cref{lem:hmin-new}. The other centres lie at
distance at least $2\sqrt2$ and contain $\VCell$ in their half-spaces by
\cref{lem:shell-new}. So $V_c\supseteq\VCell$ and
$\vol(V_c)\ge\vol(\VCell)=8$. Equality forces $V_c=\VCell$, so each of the
$24$ facet hyperplanes of $\VCell$ bounds $V_c$, and each is then the
bisector of a contact in its root direction.
\end{proof}

This is the base case that every other configuration, deviating by a
tilt angle away from a root, or having a contact radius above the
minimum, is compared against; \cref{sec:radial-new} handles the
radius freedom, and \cref{sec:cap-theorem} a deviation from a root.

\subsection{Radial reduction}\label{sec:radial-new}

\begin{lem}[Monotonicity in the contact radii]\label{lem:radial-new}
Let $u_1,\dots,u_n$ be the directions from $c$ of its active neighbours
and $d_1,\dots,d_n$ their distances. For fixed directions, the volume of
$\bigcap_i\{z:\langle z-c,u_i\rangle\le d_i/2\}$ is non-decreasing in each
$d_i$, so it is at least the volume of the same intersection with every
$d_i$ replaced by $2$. When every $d_i$ equals $2$ the directions are
pairwise at least $60^\circ$ apart and form a contact configuration
(\cref{def:contact-config}); when some $d_i$ exceeds $2$ they need not
be, and the intersection with every $d_i$ set to $2$ is then a lower
bound that belongs to no packing.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
Moving a bounding hyperplane away from $c$ can only enlarge the region it
helps bound. Two centres at distance $2$ from $c$ and at least $2$ from
each other make an angle of at least $60^\circ$ at $c$.
\end{proof}

The lemma does not say that the minimum over packing-valid radii is
attained at the all-contact corner by a contact configuration. The
corner is a lower bound but not a packing. Two centres at distance $2.4$
from $c$ and $2$ from each other are $49.25^\circ$ apart as seen from
$c$, and pulled in to distance $2$ they overlap
(\cref{fig:noncontact}(a)). Forty-eight centres at distance $2.6131$ in
the directions of the binary octahedral group, the vertices of two
$24$-cells in dual position, make a packing of $49$ balls whose central
cell has volume $19.216$; at the corner the $48$ directions have inner
products up to $0.7071$, and the cell has volume $6.594$. Hence the
reduction to contact configurations holds when every active neighbour
touches the centre, and \cref{sec:noncontact} deals with the neighbours
that do not.

For centres whose active neighbours all touch, the lemma reduces the
bound to contact configurations, which \cref{sec:deviation-domain}
treats by the number of contacts and \cref{sec:cap-theorem} by the
deviation from a root.

\section{From the local bound to the density bound}\label{sec:density-derivation}

For a packing with centre set $\mathcal{C}$, periodic or not, the density
is the upper limit
\[
  \delta(\mathcal{C})=\limsup_{R\to\infty}
  \frac{\vol(B^4)\,\#\{c\in\mathcal{C}:|c|\le R\}}{\vol(B_R)} ,
\]
where $B_R$ is the ball of radius $R$ about $0$ and $\vol(B^4)=\pi^2/2$.
A packing is \emph{saturated} if no further unit ball can be added to it
without overlap. The local bound passes to density in two
settings.\footnote{This is the classical passage from cells to density,
  on which the bounds of Blichfeldt and Rogers also rest
  \cite{Bli29,Rog64}; see \cite{Gru87} for its general form. It runs in
  one direction only, since an upper bound for the density bounds no single
  cell. The conjecture is therefore the stronger statement, and
  \cref{lem:redistribution} below asks for less.}

\begin{lem}[Cells and density]\label{lem:cells-density}
Let $v>0$, and let $\mathcal{C}$ be the centre set of a unit-ball packing of
$\mathbb{R}^4$ with $\vol(V_c)\ge v$ for every $c\in\mathcal{C}$. If
$\mathcal{C}$ is periodic, or if every cell $V_c$ lies in the ball of
radius $2$ about $c$, then $\delta(\mathcal{C})\le\vol(B^4)/v$; for $v=8$
this is $\pi^2/16$. Every cell of a saturated packing lies in the ball of
radius $2$ about its centre.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
Let $\mathcal{C}$ be periodic, with period lattice $L$ and $N$ centres in a
fundamental domain. The cells are permuted by $L$, they tile
$\mathbb{R}^4$, and the cells of one period tile a fundamental domain up to
translations by $L$; so their volumes add up to $\det L$, which is
therefore at least $vN$, and $\delta(\mathcal{C})=N\vol(B^4)/\det L\le
\vol(B^4)/v$. If every cell lies within $2$ of its centre, the
cells of the centres in $B_R$ have disjoint interiors and lie in
$B_{R+2}$, so $v\,\#\{c:|c|\le R\}\le\vol(B_{R+2})$; divide by
$\vol(B_R)$ and let $R\to\infty$. Finally, if $z\in V_c$ and
$|z-c|\ge2$, then $|z-c'|\ge|z-c|\ge2$ for every centre $c'$, and a unit
ball about $z$ can be added; so in a saturated packing $|z-c|<2$ on $V_c$.
\end{proof}

Every packing is contained in a saturated one: the packings containing
$\mathcal{C}$, ordered by inclusion, have the union of every chain as an
upper bound, since the packing condition concerns two centres at a time,
and Zorn's lemma gives a maximal element. Adding centres does not lower
the density.

\begin{cor}[The conjecture gives the density]\label{cor:conj-density}
If every Voronoi cell of every saturated unit-ball packing of
$\mathbb{R}^4$ has volume at least $8$, then $\Delta_4=\pi^2/16$.
\end{cor}

\begin{proof}
Enlarge a given packing to a saturated one and apply
\cref{lem:cells-density}; the $D_4$ lattice attains $\pi^2/16$
(\cref{lem:refcell-vol}).
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of \cref{thm:main-general}]
Every centre satisfies a hypothesis of \cref{thm:local-general} or of
\cref{thm:intro-closure}, so every cell has volume at least $8$, and
\cref{lem:cells-density} applies to a periodic or saturated packing. The
$D_4$ lattice has every cell a $24$-cell of volume $8$ and density
$\pi^2/16$.
\end{proof}

The bound need not hold cell by cell for the density to follow. Volume
can be moved between neighbouring cells, provided that what one cell
receives another gives up.

\begin{lem}[Redistribution]\label{lem:redistribution}
Let $r>0$ and $M\ge0$. Suppose that to every ordered pair of distinct
centres $c,c'$ with $|c-c'|<r$ of every saturated unit-ball packing of
$\mathbb{R}^4$ a number $\tau(c,c')$ is assigned, with
$\tau(c',c)=-\tau(c,c')$ and $|\tau(c,c')|\le M$, such that
\[
  \sigma(c)=\vol(V_c)+\sum_{c':\,0<|c'-c|<r}\tau(c,c')\ \ge\ 8
\]
at every centre $c$. Then $\Delta_4=\pi^2/16$.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
Let $\mathcal{C}$ be saturated and $N(R)=\#\{c\in\mathcal{C}:|c|\le R\}$.
By \cref{lem:cells-density} the cells of the centres in $B_R$ lie in
$B_{R+2}$, so $\sum_{|c|\le R}\vol(V_c)\le\vol(B_{R+2})$. In
$\sum_{|c|\le R}\sum_{c'}\tau(c,c')$ the terms with $|c'|\le R$ cancel in
pairs, and every other term has $R-r<|c|\le R$. The unit balls about the
centres in that shell are disjoint and lie in $B_{R+1}\setminus B_{R-r-1}$,
so there are at most $\vol(B_{R+1}\setminus B_{R-r-1})/\vol(B^4)=O(R^3)$
of them, and each has at most $\vol(B_{r+1})/\vol(B^4)$ centres within $r$.
Hence
$8N(R)\le\sum_{|c|\le R}\sigma(c)\le\vol(B_{R+2})+O(R^3)$, and
$\delta(\mathcal{C})\le\pi^2/16$ follows as in \cref{lem:cells-density}
(\cref{fig:saturation}). Every packing lies in a saturated one.
\end{proof}

\begin{figure}[tb]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.62\textwidth]{fig_tikz_saturation}
\caption{The counting of \cref{lem:cells-density,lem:redistribution},
drawn in the plane for a saturated packing of unit discs, to scale. Every
cell lies within $2$ of its centre, so the cells of the centres in $B_R$
(blue) lie in $B_{R+2}$. In the sum of the transfers over the centres in
$B_R$, every pair inside $B_R$ cancels; what remains are the pairs drawn in
orange, from a centre in the shell $R-r<|c|\le R$ (green) to a centre
outside $B_R$, and their number grows like the area of the shell, one
power of $R$ below the number of centres.}
\label{fig:saturation}
\end{figure}

With $\tau=0$ the hypothesis is the twenty-four-cell conjecture, so
\cref{lem:redistribution} asks for less: a centre at which $\vol(V_c)<8$ is
allowed, provided that its neighbours have volume to spare and pass it on.
The theorems of this paper give $\sigma(c)=\vol(V_c)\ge8$ with $\tau=0$ at
every centre that \cref{thm:local-general,thm:intro-closure} cover, so a
transfer is needed only towards the centres of \cref{conj:shell}
(\cref{sec:status}).

A hypothesis on the centres of a packing need not survive the enlargement
to a saturated packing, since the added centres change the neighbourhoods
of the old ones; this is why \cref{thm:main-general} is stated for packings
that are periodic or already saturated, while \cref{cor:conj-density} needs
the local bound at every centre of every saturated packing, which is the
twenty-four-cell conjecture.

\section{Positive kernels and certificates}\label{sec:kernels}

Several theorems of this paper bound a quantity over every finite
configuration of points that keep a minimal distance from one another.
Some bound the number of points of $S^3$ whose pairwise inner products are
at most a given $t$ (\cref{thm:m24,thm:kissing-stable,thm:twenty-six}).
The others bound a sum, over the points and their pairs, of explicit
functions of distances and angles; the main example is the volume $U(Y)$
of a union of caps, which decides the cell volume once no three caps meet
(\cref{thm:certificate,thm:m23-labelled,thm:count31,thm:count30,thm:count29,prop:levels}).
All of these are proved in the same way, and each proof has two halves of
different kinds. The first half is analysis. A kernel that is positive by
a theorem turns the statement about all configurations into an inequality
between explicit functions of one, two or three points, and that
inequality has to hold on a compact domain of a few real variables. The
second half decides the inequality, which no longer mentions
configurations, by finitely many evaluations, each one either exact or
enclosed with outward rounding. This section proves the first half once,
in the generality in which the later sections use it, says what the
second half has to establish and why finitely many evaluations establish
it, and lists the certificates of the paper in \cref{tab:certificates}.
The proofs that follow cite it, and none of them depends on the way in
which a certificate was found.

\subsection{Positivity on the three-sphere}\label{sec:kernels-positivity}

For $k\ge0$ let $U_k$ be the Chebyshev polynomial of the second kind and
$G_k=U_k/(k+1)$, so that
$G_k(\cos\theta)=\sin((k+1)\theta)/((k+1)\sin\theta)$ and $G_k(1)=1$. It
is the Gegenbauer polynomial of $S^3$: the functions on $S^3$ that are
harmonic polynomials of degree $k$ and invariant under the rotations
fixing a point $e$ are the multiples of $G_k(\langle\cdot,e\rangle)$.

\begin{lem}[Zonal positivity]\label{lem:zonal-psd}
Let $W\subset S^3$ be finite and $a\colon W\to\mathbb{R}$. Then
$\sum_{x,x'\in W}a(x)\,a(x')\,G_k(\langle x,x'\rangle)\ge0$ for every
$k\ge0$.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
Let $\mathcal H_k$ be the space of restrictions to $S^3$ of the harmonic
homogeneous polynomials of degree $k$ on $\mathbb{R}^4$, which has
dimension $(k+1)^2$, and let $(Y_m)$ be a basis of it that is orthonormal
for the surface measure $\sigma$, of total mass $2\pi^2$. The kernel
$Z(x,x')=\sum_mY_m(x)Y_m(x')$ does not depend on the choice of the basis,
so it is invariant under the orthogonal group, and it is therefore a
function $g(\langle x,x'\rangle)$ of the inner product alone, a
polynomial of degree at most $k$. For fixed $x'=e$ it lies in
$\mathcal H_k$, so it is orthogonal on $S^3$ to every polynomial of lower
degree, and in particular to $h(\langle x,e\rangle)$ whenever
$\deg h<k$. The measure $\sigma$ carries $u=\langle x,e\rangle$ to a
multiple of $(1-u^2)^{1/2}\,du$ on $[-1,1]$, so $g$ is orthogonal to the
polynomials of lower degree for that weight, and the orthogonal
polynomials of that weight are the $U_k$; hence $g$ is a multiple of
$G_k$. On the diagonal, $Z(x,x)=g(1)$ is a constant whose integral over
$S^3$ is $\dim\mathcal H_k=(k+1)^2$, so $g(1)=(k+1)^2/2\pi^2$ and
\[
  \sum_mY_m(x)\,Y_m(x')=\frac{(k+1)^2}{2\pi^2}\,G_k(\langle x,x'\rangle),
\]
which is the addition theorem. The sum in the lemma is therefore
\[
  \frac{2\pi^2}{(k+1)^2}\sum_m\Bigl(\sum_{x\in W}a(x)\,Y_m(x)\Bigr)^2\;\ge\;0 .\qedhere
\]
\end{proof}

This is the case of $S^3$ of Schoenberg's theorem \cite{Sch42}, and with
$a=1$ it is the linear programming bound of Delsarte, Goethals and Seidel
\cite{DGS77} in its primal form. The kernels of this paper also carry, at
each point, a vector of numbers attached to it, most often the values of
a few polynomials at the distance of a centre from $c$, and the same
argument applies to them.

\begin{lem}[Kernels with labels]\label{lem:labelled-psd}
Let $W$ be a finite set, let $\xi\colon W\to S^3$ and
$p\colon W\to\mathbb{R}^{r+1}$ be any maps, and let $A$ be a positive
semidefinite matrix of order $r+1$. Then
\[
  \sum_{x,x'\in W}G_k(\langle\xi(x),\xi(x')\rangle)\;p(x)^{\mathsf T}A\,p(x')\;\ge\;0
\]
for every $k\ge0$, and for $k=0$ the sum is $v^{\mathsf T}Av$ with
$v=\sum_{x\in W}p(x)$.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
Write $A=\sum_j\lambda_je_je_j^{\mathsf T}$ with $\lambda_j\ge0$. The sum
is
\[
  \sum_j\lambda_j\sum_{x,x'\in W}a_j(x)\,a_j(x')\,G_k(\langle\xi(x),\xi(x')\rangle),
  \qquad a_j(x)=e_j\cdot p(x),
\]
and each inner sum is nonnegative by
\cref{lem:zonal-psd}, applied to the image of $\xi$ with the weights of
points that $\xi$ sends to the same direction added together. For $k=0$
the claim is $G_0=1$.
\end{proof}

Triples of points are handled by the matrices of Bachoc and Vallentin
\cite{BV08}. The three-point matrices are built from the Legendre
polynomials $P_k$, which play the same role on $S^2$. Fix a degree $d$
and, for $0\le k\le d$, let $Y_k(u,v,t)$ be the square matrix of order
$d-k+1$ with entries
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Yk}
\begin{aligned}
  Y_k(u,v,t)_{ij}&=T_i(u)\,T_j(v)\,
  \bigl((1-u^2)(1-v^2)\bigr)^{k/2}\\
  &\quad\times P_k\Bigl(\frac{t-uv}{\sqrt{(1-u^2)(1-v^2)}}\Bigr),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
for $0\le i,j\le d-k$,
where $T_i$ is the Chebyshev polynomial of the first kind; since $P_k$
has the parity of $k$, each entry is a polynomial in $u,v,t$. Bachoc
and Vallentin write $u^iv^j$ in place of $T_i(u)T_j(v)$; the two
families differ by a fixed congruence of the matrices and are
interchangeable in everything below, and the Chebyshev basis is the
better conditioned one on $[-1,\tfrac12]$. Let $S_k$ be the average of
$Y_k$ over the six permutations of $(u,v,t)$.

\begin{lem}[Positivity of the three-point matrices]\label{lem:three-point-psd}
For every finite $W\subset S^3$ and every $k$, the matrix
$\sum_{(x,y,z)\in W^3}S_k(\langle x,y\rangle,\langle x,z\rangle,\langle y,z\rangle)$
is positive semidefinite.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
Fix $e\in S^3$ and write $x^\perp=x-\langle e,x\rangle e$ for $x\in S^3$.
With $u=\langle e,x\rangle$, $v=\langle e,y\rangle$, $t=\langle x,y\rangle$
one has $|x^\perp|^2=1-u^2$ and $\langle x^\perp,y^\perp\rangle=t-uv$, so
the scalar factor of \eqref{eq:Yk} is
$Q_k(x,y)=|x^\perp|^k|y^\perp|^kP_k(\langle x^\perp,y^\perp\rangle/(|x^\perp||y^\perp|))$,
read as $0$ for $k\ge1$ and as $1$ for $k=0$ when one of the projections
vanishes. The kernel $P_k(\langle\xi,\eta\rangle)$ is positive definite
on the unit sphere of $e^\perp$, a copy of $S^2$, by \cite{Sch42}, and
multiplying a positive definite kernel by $a(x)a(y)$ keeps it positive
definite; so $Q_k$ is positive definite on $S^3$, and for any functions
$a_0,\dots,a_{d-k}$ the matrix with entries
$\sum_{x,y\in W}a_i(x)a_j(y)Q_k(x,y)$ is positive semidefinite, because
$c^{\mathsf T}Mc=\sum_{x,y}b(x)Q_k(x,y)b(y)$ with $b=\sum_ic_ia_i$. Taking
$a_i(x)=T_i(\langle e,x\rangle)$ gives
$\sum_{x,y\in W}Y_k(\langle e,x\rangle,\langle e,y\rangle,\langle x,y\rangle)\succeq0$
for every $e$; summing over $e\in W$ gives the same for the sum over
$W^3$ of $Y_k$, and that sum is unchanged by permuting the three
arguments, so it equals the sum of $S_k$.
\end{proof}

In \cref{sec:noncontact} each point also carries a type, the range in
which the distance of its centre from $c$ lies, and the three-point
matrices are then taken block by block over the types.

\begin{lem}[Triples of typed directions]\label{lem:typed-triples}
Let $W\subset S^3$ be finite, and let each $x\in W$ carry a type $\tau(x)$
from a finite set $\mathcal T$. For $0\le k\le d$ and $e,x,y\in S^3$ let
$Y^\tau_k(e;x,y)$ be the square matrix indexed by the pairs $(s,i)$ with
$s\in\mathcal T$ and $0\le i\le d-k$ whose entry in row $(s,i)$ and column
$(s',j)$ is $Y_k(\langle e,x\rangle,\langle e,y\rangle,\langle x,y\rangle)_{ij}$,
with $Y_k$ as in \eqref{eq:Yk}, if $\tau(x)=s$ and $\tau(y)=s'$, and $0$
otherwise. Let $f_1,\dots,f_d$ be positive semidefinite matrices indexed by
$\mathcal T$, and for each $s\in\mathcal T$ let $F^s_0,\dots,F^s_d$ be
positive semidefinite matrices of the orders of $Y^\tau_0,\dots,Y^\tau_d$.
Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:typed-triples}
  \sum_{x,y\in W}\sum_{k=1}^dG_k(\langle x,y\rangle)\,(f_k)_{\tau(x)\tau(y)}
  +\sum_{e,x,y\in W}\sum_{k=0}^d\bigl\langle F^{\tau(e)}_k,\,Y^\tau_k(e;x,y)\bigr\rangle\ \ge\ 0 .
\end{equation}
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
Write $f_k=\sum_jv_jv_j^{\mathsf T}$. Then the first sum is
$\sum_k\sum_j\sum_{x,y}v_j(\tau(x))\,G_k(\langle x,y\rangle)\,v_j(\tau(y))$,
and each inner sum is nonnegative by \cref{lem:zonal-psd}. Fix $e\in W$ and $k$. The proof of
\cref{lem:three-point-psd}, run with the functions
$a_{(s,i)}(x)=T_i(\langle e,x\rangle)$ for $\tau(x)=s$ and
$a_{(s,i)}(x)=0$ otherwise, shows that $\sum_{x,y\in W}Y^\tau_k(e;x,y)$ is
positive semidefinite, so its inner product with $F^{\tau(e)}_k$ is
nonnegative. Summing over $e$ and $k$ gives the second sum.
\end{proof}

Split the left side of \eqref{eq:typed-triples} according to which of the
points coincide. The terms with $x=y$ in the first sum and $e=x=y$ in the
second add up to $\sum_{x\in W}q_{\tau(x)}$, with one constant $q_s$ for
each type. The terms in which exactly two points of $W$ occur, $x\ne y$ in
the first sum and $\{e,x,y\}=\{x',y'\}$ in the second, add up for each
unordered pair $\{x',y'\}$ to a polynomial
$Q_{\tau(x')\tau(y')}(\langle x',y'\rangle)$, and the terms with $e,x,y$
distinct add up for each set of three points to a polynomial
$R_{\tau\tau'\tau''}$ of their three inner products.\footnote{Write
  $\mathbf t_m(u)=(T_0(u),\dots,T_m(u))$ and $F^s_k[s',s'']$ for the block
  of $F^s_k$ in the rows of type $s'$ and the columns of type $s''$. Then
  $q_s=\sum_{k=1}^d(f_k)_{ss}+\mathbf t_d(1)^{\mathsf T}F^s_0[s,s]\,\mathbf t_d(1)$ and
  \begin{align*}
  Q_{st}(u)&=\textstyle2\sum_{k=1}^dG_k(u)(f_k)_{st}
   +2\,\mathbf t_d(1)^{\mathsf T}\bigl(F^s_0[s,t]+F^t_0[t,s]\bigr)\mathbf t_d(u)\\
   &\quad\textstyle+\sum_{k=0}^d(1-u^2)^k\,\mathbf t_{d-k}(u)^{\mathsf T}\bigl(F^s_k[t,t]+F^t_k[s,s]\bigr)\mathbf t_{d-k}(u).
  \end{align*}
  The second term collects the triples in which the pole $e$ is one of the
  two points and $x,y$ are the two points in either order; only $k=0$
  survives there, because the scalar factor of \eqref{eq:Yk} vanishes at
  $\langle e,x\rangle=1$ for $k\ge1$. The third collects the triples with
  $x=y\ne e$, where that factor is $(1-u^2)^k$. Finally $R$ is the sum,
  over the three choices of the pole among the three points, of
  $2\sum_k\langle F^{\tau(e)}_k,Y^\tau_k(e;x,y)\rangle$ with $x,y$ the
  other two; it does not change when two points of the same type are
  exchanged together with their inner products.}
Hence every finite set of typed points of $S^3$ satisfies
\begin{equation}\label{eq:typed-split}
  0\ \le\ \sum_{x}q_{\tau(x)}+\sum_{\{x,y\}}Q_{\tau(x)\tau(y)}(\langle x,y\rangle)
  +\sum_{\{x,y,z\}}R_{\tau(x)\tau(y)\tau(z)}(\langle x,y\rangle,\langle x,z\rangle,\langle y,z\rangle).
\end{equation}

\subsection{From a positive kernel to an inequality}\label{sec:kernels-template}

The next theorem is the step that every bound on a union of caps in this
paper takes. It holds for any numbers $s_y$ and $\pi_{yy'}$; the geometry
enters only when they are chosen.

\begin{thm}[Two-point certificates]\label{thm:two-point-template}
Let $\mathcal D\subset\mathbb{R}^4\setminus\{0\}$ and
$p\colon\mathcal D\to\mathbb{R}^{r+1}$, let $A_0,\dots,A_D$ be symmetric
matrices of order $r+1$ with $A_1,\dots,A_D$ positive semidefinite, and
put
\[
  K(y,y')=\sum_{k=0}^DG_k(\langle\hat y,\hat y'\rangle)\;p(y)^{\mathsf T}A_k\,p(y'),
  \qquad\hat y=y/|y| .
\]
Suppose that for some $z\in\mathbb{R}^{r+1}$ and $t\in\mathbb{R}$ the
matrix $\begin{pmatrix}A_0&z\\ z^{\mathsf T}&t\end{pmatrix}$ is positive
semidefinite. Then for every finite $Y\subset\mathcal D$ and all real
numbers $s_y$, for $y\in Y$, and $\pi_{yy'}$, for $\{y,y'\}\subset Y$,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:two-point-template}
  \sum_{y\in Y}s_y-\sum_{\{y,y'\}\subset Y}\pi_{yy'}
  \;\le\;\sum_{y\in Y}\Bigl(s_y+\tfrac12K(y,y)-z\cdot p(y)\Bigr)+\frac t2
  +\sum_{\{y,y'\}\subset Y}\bigl(K(y,y')-\pi_{yy'}\bigr).
\end{equation}
In particular, if $K(y,y')\le\pi_{yy'}$ for every pair and
$s_y+\frac12K(y,y)-z\cdot p(y)\le m$ for every $y$, then the left side is
at most $|Y|\,m+\frac t2$.
\end{thm}

\begin{proof}
Put $v=\sum_{y\in Y}p(y)$. By \cref{lem:labelled-psd}, with
$\xi(y)=\hat y$, the terms of degree $k\ge1$ add a nonnegative amount to
$\sum_{y,y'\in Y}K(y,y')$, and the term of degree $0$ adds
$v^{\mathsf T}A_0v$. Hence
\[
  2\sum_{\{y,y'\}}K(y,y')=\sum_{y,y'\in Y}K(y,y')-\sum_{y\in Y}K(y,y)
  \;\ge\;v^{\mathsf T}A_0v-\sum_{y\in Y}K(y,y).
\]
The quadratic form of the positive semidefinite matrix, evaluated at the
vector $(v,-1)$, gives $v^{\mathsf T}A_0v-2z\cdot v+t\ge0$. The two
together give
$-\sum_{\{y,y'\}}K(y,y')\le\frac12\sum_yK(y,y)-z\cdot v+\frac t2$, and
adding $\sum_ys_y+\sum_{\{y,y'\}}(K(y,y')-\pi_{yy'})$ to both sides gives
\eqref{eq:two-point-template}, since $z\cdot v=\sum_yz\cdot p(y)$.
\end{proof}

In \cref{sec:noncontact} the theorem is applied with $s_y=S(|y|)$, the
volume of the cap that the centre $y$ cuts from $B(c,\sqrt{3/2})$, and
$\pi_{yy'}$ the volume of the overlap of two caps, so that the left side
is the volume $U(Y)$ of their union; and $p(y)$ is the vector of
Chebyshev polynomials of a rescaled distance $|y|$, which is how the
kernel carries the distance as a label. The hypothesis $K\le\pi$ on the
pairs is then an inequality between two functions of three real
variables, the two distances and the inner product of the directions, on
the region that the packing condition $|y-y'|\ge2$ allows, and the bound
on $s_y+\frac12K(y,y)-z\cdot p(y)$ is an inequality in one variable. Three
further forms of the same argument occur.

\emph{Triples with types.} Adding \eqref{eq:typed-split} to
\eqref{eq:two-point-template} changes the pair terms to
$K-\pi+Q$ and adds a sum of the polynomials $R$ over the triples. Once
each of these is bounded above by a constant for each combination of
types, the right side depends on $Y$ only through the number of its
centres in each of finitely many ranges of distance, and the bound
becomes a maximisation over finitely many integer vectors
(\eqref{eq:thirty-bound} in the proof of \cref{thm:count30}).

\emph{Counting points.} With constants in place of $s_y$ and $\pi$, the
positivity of \cref{lem:zonal-psd,lem:three-point-psd}, summed over all
ordered pairs and triples of a code and separated by the points that
coincide, bounds the number of its points; this is the form of the proofs
of \cref{thm:kissing-stable,thm:twenty-six}, and with $N$ fixed and a weight
on the pairs it is \cref{lem:certificate}.

\emph{Subsets of two points.} The classification of twenty-four contacts
uses a kernel on the subsets of at most two points of $S^3$, positive in
the sense of \cref{sec:las2}; \cref{lem:las2} turns it into a bound on the
size of a code, and the equality case of that bound identifies the code.

\subsection{What a certificate must satisfy, and how it is checked}\label{sec:kernels-check}

A certificate is a finite list of rational numbers: the coefficients of
$p$, the entries of the matrices $A_k$, of $z$ and of $t$, and, where
triples enter, the entries of the matrices of
\cref{lem:three-point-psd,lem:typed-triples}. Two kinds of statement
have to be proved about it. The first is that certain symmetric rational
matrices are positive semidefinite. The second is that certain explicit
functions of at most three real variables, built from the certificate and
from the geometric functions of the problem, stay below stated constants
on stated compact domains. The geometric functions are the volume $S$ of a
cap and the volume $\Pi$ of the overlap of two caps
(\cref{sec:noncontact}), and the weight $\omega$ of the pair angles
(\cref{sec:certificate}). Both are decided by finitely many
operations, and the next two lemmas say why that is enough.

\begin{lem}[Exact positivity]\label{lem:exact-psd}
Let $A$ be a symmetric rational matrix. If $A=LDL^{\mathsf T}$ with $L$
unit lower triangular and $D$ diagonal with nonnegative entries, then $A$
is positive semidefinite, and positive definite if the entries of $D$ are
positive. If $A$ is positive definite and
$t\ge z^{\mathsf T}A^{-1}z$, then
$\begin{pmatrix}A&z\\ z^{\mathsf T}&t\end{pmatrix}$ is positive
semidefinite.
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
For the first statement, $x^{\mathsf T}Ax=\sum_id_i\bigl((L^{\mathsf T}x)_i\bigr)^2\ge0$,
and when every $d_i$ is positive this vanishes only if $L^{\mathsf T}x=0$,
that is if $x=0$, since $L$ is invertible. For the second,
\[
  \begin{pmatrix}A&z\\ z^{\mathsf T}&t\end{pmatrix}
  =\begin{pmatrix}I&0\\ z^{\mathsf T}A^{-1}&1\end{pmatrix}
   \begin{pmatrix}A&0\\ 0&t-z^{\mathsf T}A^{-1}z\end{pmatrix}
   \begin{pmatrix}I&A^{-1}z\\ 0&1\end{pmatrix},
\]
a congruence with a block diagonal matrix whose blocks are positive
semidefinite.
\end{proof}

The factorisation is carried out over $\mathbb{Q}$, so the test involves
no rounding. It is applied to every matrix of the certificates of
\cref{thm:certificate,thm:kissing-stable,thm:twenty-six,thm:count31,thm:count30,thm:count29},
and the number $t$ of \cref{thm:two-point-template} is taken to be
$z^{\mathsf T}A_0^{-1}z$ rounded up, so that the second statement gives
the positivity of the block matrix exactly.

\begin{lem}[Enclosures on a box]\label{lem:enclosures}
Let $R=\prod_{i=1}^n[a_i,b_i]$ be a box with centre $c$ and half-widths
$h_i=\frac12(b_i-a_i)$.
\begin{enumerate}[label=\textup{(\roman*)},leftmargin=2.2em]
\item Let $P$ be a polynomial of degree at most $\delta_i$ in its $i$-th
variable, written $P=\sum_\beta c_\beta B_\beta$ in the tensor Bernstein
basis
$B_\beta(x)=\prod_i\binom{\delta_i}{\beta_i}(x_i-a_i)^{\beta_i}(b_i-x_i)^{\delta_i-\beta_i}/(b_i-a_i)^{\delta_i}$.
Then $\min_\beta c_\beta\le P\le\max_\beta c_\beta$ on $R$.
\item Let $f$ be twice continuously differentiable near $R$, with
$|\partial_i\partial_jf|\le M_{ij}$ on $R$. Then on $R$
\[
  f\;\le\;f(c)+\sum_i|\partial_if(c)|\,h_i+\tfrac12\sum_{i,j}M_{ij}h_ih_j ,
\]
and the same bound with the signs of the last two terms reversed holds
from below.
\item Let $f$ be nondecreasing in some of its variables and nonincreasing
in the others. Then its largest value on $R$ is taken at the corner with
the upper ends of the first variables and the lower ends of the others,
and its least value at the opposite corner.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lem}

\begin{proof}
(i) The $B_\beta$ are nonnegative on $R$ and add up to $1$ there, by the
binomial theorem in each variable, so $P$ is a convex combination of its
coefficients at every point of $R$. (ii) By Taylor's theorem with the
remainder in Lagrange form along the segment from $c$ to $x$,
$f(x)=f(c)+\nabla f(c)\cdot(x-c)+\frac12(x-c)^{\mathsf T}\nabla^2f(\zeta)(x-c)$
for some $\zeta\in R$, and $|x_i-c_i|\le h_i$. (iii) Move the variables
one at a time to the end of their intervals at which $f$ does not
decrease.
\end{proof}

Every enclosure is evaluated either in exact rational arithmetic or in
interval or ball arithmetic with outward rounding
\cite[Ch.~5]{MKC09},\cite{Joh17}, so that the number computed is at least
the true upper bound. The domain of an inequality is a box, or a
semialgebraic part of one: the pairs allowed by $|y-y'|\ge2$, which in
the distances and the inner product is the condition
$\langle\hat y,\hat y'\rangle\le a(|y|,|y'|)$ of \eqref{eq:amax}, or the
triples of inner products whose Gram matrix is positive semidefinite. It
is covered by finitely many boxes, and on each box either an exact test or
an enclosure of the defining condition shows that the box misses the
domain, or one of the enclosures of \cref{lem:enclosures} shows that the
function stays below its constant there. Since every point of the domain
lies in a box of the second kind, the inequality holds on the whole
domain. The boxes are produced by repeated bisection
(\cref{app:box-cover-algo}), and that their union is the box one started
from is checked on their boundaries in exact arithmetic; the counts
quoted are those of the final covers. Polynomials built from the
certificate are bounded by (i) or (ii). The functions $S$, $\Pi$ and
$\omega$ are not polynomials. They enter through (iii), by the
monotonicity of $S$ and of $\Pi$ (\cref{lem:pair-closed}), with the
corner values computed from their closed forms in ball arithmetic, or
through (ii) with tables of rigorous bounds for $\omega$ and its first
two derivatives. Floating point is used only to find a certificate; once
its entries are fixed as the rationals they denote, nothing that follows
depends on how they were obtained, and a numerical optimum that has not
been made into a certificate in this way is never used as a bound.

\cref{thm:kissing-stable,thm:twenty-six,thm:count31,thm:count30,thm:count29}
